# Abstract

This gazetteer compiles coordinates, historical names and benchmark
population estimates for 976 settlements across the Russian Core and the
Middle Volga (~1,94M km²) from 10000BC to 1900AD. It accompanies the
regional demographic survey of the eight principalities by tracking
individual camps, hillforts, villages and towns from Mesolithic peat-bog
sites (such as Nizhneye Veretye I and Zamostje 2) and Early Iron Age
Dyakovo and Gorodets bluff forts to the provincial capitals and
industrial cities of the late Russian Empire.

Before the Mongol invasion of 1236-1240AD, urban life peaked in the
great river trading capitals of Bilyar (50k), Veliky Novgorod (28k),
Smolensk (25k), Vladimir-on-Klyazma (22k) and Old Ryazan (18k). After
the thirteenth-century sack of the southern and eastern capitals, town
growth shifted during the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries to the
Golden Horde mint cities of Bolgar (40k) and Mukhshi and to the rising
appanage seats of Moscow (40k by 1450AD) and Tver. From the sixteenth to
the nineteenth centuries, the founding of southern fortress towns along
the Belgorod and Simbirsk lines, the building of Saint Petersburg after
1703 and late nineteenth-century railway and textile expansion carried
Saint Petersburg to 1,26M and Moscow to 1,04M in the 1897 census.

# Methodology

This gazetteer sets out the locations, historical names and benchmark
population estimates for 976 settlements across the eight historical
regions of the Russian Core and the Middle Volga: Novgorod and Pskov,
Vladimir-Suzdal and Rostov-Yaroslavl, Moscow and Tver, Ryazan and the
Upper Don, Smolensk and the Upper Dnieper, Chernigov-Bryansk and the
Upper Oka, Nizhny Novgorod, Vyatka and Great Perm, and the Middle Volga
and Cis-Urals.

Population figures are drawn from excavated house floors and settlement
plans for Mesolithic, Neolithic, Bronze Age and Early Iron Age sites
[\[1\]](#ref-Zhilin2019)[\[2\]](#ref-Oshibkina2006)[\[3\]](#ref-Kostyleva2014)[\[4\]](#ref-Kostyleva2022)[\[5\]](#ref-Nordqvist2020)[\[6\]](#ref-Engovatova2021),
walled town areas (*detinets* and *okolny gorod*) and outer craft
quarters (*posad*) for Kievan Rus, Volga Bulgaria and Golden Horde towns
[\[7\]](#ref-Tikhomirov1956)[\[8\]](#ref-Kuza1989)[\[9\]](#ref-Kuza1996)[\[10\]](#ref-Nosov1990)[\[11\]](#ref-Darkevich1995)[\[12\]](#ref-Eniosova2012)[\[13\]](#ref-Khuzin2001)[\[14\]](#ref-Rudenko2012)[\[15\]](#ref-Nedashkovsky2000)[\[16\]](#ref-Belorybkin2022)[\[17\]](#ref-Belavin2023),
and Muscovite tax books (*pistsovye knigi*), Imperial poll-tax revisions
(*revizii*) and the 1897 census for early modern and nineteenth-century
towns
[\[18\]](#ref-Kopanev1959)[\[19\]](#ref-Shapiro1971)[\[20\]](#ref-Vodarsky1973)[\[21\]](#ref-Vodarsky1977)[\[22\]](#ref-Kabuzan1963)[\[23\]](#ref-Kabuzan1971)[\[24\]](#ref-Rashin1956)[\[25\]](#ref-Mironov2000)[\[26\]](#ref-Troynitsky1905).

Populations of medieval towns and large villages at five benchmark
windows, contrasting the pre-Mongol peak (1200 - 1235AD) at Bilyar,
Veliky Novgorod, Smolensk, Vladimir and Old Ryazan with the fourteenth-
and fifteenth-century recovery under the appanage princes and Golden
Horde khans.

Town populations across six early modern and imperial benchmarks,
showing the seventeenth-century founding of southern frontier forts
along the Belgorod and Simbirsk lines, the rise of Saint Petersburg
after 1703 and late nineteenth-century railway and factory growth.

Master chronological list of all 976 settlements in the Russian Core and
the Middle Volga, pairing decimal coordinates and occupied date spans
with the full sequence of population estimates from 10000BC to 1900AD.
